On March 30, 2021, Capitol Police officers James Blassingame and Sidney Hemby filed suit against Trump for physical and emotional injuries they suffered during the January 6 attack. They later amended their complaint, adding violation of the Ku Klux Klan Act and conspiracy to interfere with civil rights.[^cite_note-Pending-284]

On August 13, 2021, the widow of [Police Officer Jeffrey L. Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Jeffrey_L._Smith) and the Estate of [Jeffrey L. Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_L._Smith) sued David Walls-Kaufman and Taylor Taranto for wrongful death, assault, battery, and aiding and abetting.[^cite_note-285] Smith's counsel [David P. Weber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_P._Weber) enlisted the assistance of online open source intelligence investigators to locate and identify the alleged assailants.[^cite_note-286] The civil lawsuit was filed in the United States District Court for the District of Columbia.[^cite_note-287] Both men were also criminally charged. In June 2022, Walls-Kaufman was arrested for his behavior during the Capitol riot, and in June 2023, he was sentenced to 60 days in prison.[^cite_note-288] He is covered by Trump's January 2025 mass pardon of the rioters. Taranto was criminally charged for bringing weapons near former President Obama's home in 2023,[^cite_note-289] and as Trump's mass pardon of the rioters doesn't cover that offense, he is likely to remain in prison.[^cite_note-290]

On October 18, 2021, Trump filed a lawsuit against Thompson, the United States House Select Committee to Investigate the January 6 Attack on the United States Capitol, [David Ferriero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Ferriero) (Archivist of the National Archives) and the [National Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Archives_and_Records_Administration), seeking an injunction against the release of records related to communications made with the Trump administration on the day of the attack. The lawsuit claims the request and the committee are partisan shams and illegitimate.[^cite_note-291][^cite_note-292] The lawsuit was eventually tossed after the Supreme Court denied a [certiorari petition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Certiorari) on February 22, 2022, allowing the select committee access to the documents.[^cite_note-293]

On December 14, 2021, [Washington D.C. Attorney General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney_General_for_the_District_of_Columbia) [Karl Racine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Racine) filed a lawsuit in District of Columbia's federal court, seeking damages from the Proud Boys and the Oath Keepers, claiming that both groups conspired to overturn the results of the 2020 in a coordinated act of [domestic terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_terrorism_in_the_United_States). The lawsuit seeks monetary damages for injuries, property damage, and additional costs.[^cite_note-294]

Judge [Amit Mehta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amit_Mehta) consolidated three of the civil suits before him—[Thompson v. Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thompson_v._Trump), Swalwell v. Trump, and Blassingame v. Trump—and held oral arguments on January 10, 2022, to consider whether Trump and the other defendants were immune from liability. The defendants had requested immunity on the grounds of the First Amendment, and those who were elected officials also claimed immunity based on that status. They asked the judge to dismiss the lawsuits. The judge, however, observed that Trump took no action for two hours on January 6. A "reasonable person" would have clarified his own message to stop the violence, the judge argued, yet Trump failed to "denounce the conduct immediately...and sent a tweet that arguably exacerbated things".[^cite_note-liability-295][^cite_note-296] On February 18, 2022, Mehta denied the motion to dismiss some of the claims, while granting the motion to dismiss other of the claims. Trump then appealed the cases to the [District of Columbia Circuit Court of Appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Court_of_Appeals) on July 27, 2022, claiming absolute immunity.[^cite_note-Pending-284] The Court of Appeals ruled against him on December 1, 2023, allowing the civil suits to proceed.[^cite_note-297]

Judge Mehta was scheduled to hear arguments for six more lawsuits related to January 6.[^cite_note-liability-295]

In November 2023, Jason Donner sued [Fox News](11121.md) for firing him in September 2022. Donner had reported from inside the Capitol on January 6, 2021; as the building came under attack, he called the Fox News control room and complained that the network was "gonna get us all killed" if they kept making on-air claims downplaying the riot.[^cite_note-298]

District Judge Sarah Wallace in November 2023 ruled that "Trump incited an insurrection on January 6, 2021", because he "acted with the specific intent to incite political violence and direct it at the Capitol with the purpose of disrupting the electoral certification". Wallace cited as relevant Trump's "history of courting extremists", his years-long effort of having "embraced the virtue and necessity of political violence", and his awareness that "his supporters were willing to engage in political violence" on his behalf. Wallace then quoted inflammatory rhetoric from Trump from January 6, 2021, where he said that this was "the most corrupt election in the history, maybe of the world", so his supporters could, as a "matter of national security", follow "very different rules" and "fight like hell", or they would not "have a country anymore". Despite this finding, Wallace declined to disqualify Trump from the Colorado primary ballot because Wallace ruled that the president is not an [officer of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Officer_of_the_United_States) covered by the [Fourteenth Amendment to the United States Constitution](31666.md).[^cite_note-299][^cite_note-300]

## Potential spread of COVID-19

Main article: [COVID-19 pandemic in Washington, D.C. § Concerns in relation to the U.S. Capitol attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Washington,_D.C.#Concerns_in_relation_to_the_U.S._Capitol_attack)

Public health experts have said that the riot was a potential [COVID-19](63030231.md) [superspreader event](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superspreader_event).[^cite_note-Storming-301] [Anthony Fauci](1227250.md), Director of the [National Institute of Allergy and Infectious Diseases](1013155.md) and lead member of the [White House Coronavirus Task Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Coronavirus_Task_Force), said that the rioters' failure to "adhere to the fundamentals of public health" to prevent the spread of COVID-19—such as "universal wearing of masks, keeping [physical distance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_distancing), \[and] avoiding crowds in congregate settings"—placed them at risk.[^cite_note-302] The day after the event, Eric Toner, a senior scholar from the [Johns Hopkins Center for Health Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johns_Hopkins_Center_for_Health_Security), said the attack was "extraordinarily dangerous" from a public-health perspective.[^cite_note-Storming-301]

## Impact and legacy

### Contemporary analysis and terminology

See also: [Description of the attempts to overturn the election as an attempted coup](65905202.md#Description_as_attempted_coup)

A week following the attack, journalists were searching for an appropriate word to describe the event.[^cite_note-303] According to the Associated Press, U.S. media outlets first described the developments on January 6 as "a rally or protest", but as the events of the day escalated and further reporting and images emerged, the descriptions shifted to "an assault, a riot, an insurrection, domestic terrorism or even a [coup](25914154.md) attempt".[^cite_note-Bauder-304] It was variably observed that the media outlets were settling on the terms "riot" and "insurrection".[^cite_note-Bauder-304][^cite_note-305]

According to [NPR](29697232.md), "By definition, 'insurrection', and its derivative, 'insurgency', are accurate. 'Riot' and 'mob' are equally correct. While these words are not interchangeable, they are all suitable when describing Jan. 6."[^cite_note-306] *The New York Times* assessed the event as having brought the United States "hours away from a full-blown constitutional crisis".[^cite_note-307] [Brian Stelter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Stelter) in *CNN Business* wrote that the events of the Capitol attack "will be remembered as an act of domestic terrorism against the United States".[^cite_note-308] Although several of the rioters were charged with the crime of "[seditious conspiracy](26851299.md)", none of them were criminally charged with the crime of "[insurrection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confiscation_Act_of_1862#Insurrection_or_rebellion)", including Trump.[^cite_note-insur1-309][^cite_note-insur2-310]

The attack was widely described as an attempted [coup d'état](25914154.md)[^cite_note-311][^cite_note-312][^cite_note-coup_d'état-313] or [self-coup](1221872.md).[^cite_note-ProvostHarvey-314][^cite_note-Self-coup_journal-315][^cite_note-316] Federal judge [David Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_O._Carter) described Trump's actions as "a coup in search of a legal theory".[^cite_note-Broadwater_Feuer_Haberman_3/28/2022-317] [Naunihal Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naunihal_Singh_\(academic\)) of the U.S. [Naval War College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_War_College), and author of *Seizing Power: The Strategic Logic of Military Coups*, wrote that the attack on the Capitol was "an insurrection, a violent uprising against the government" and "sedition", but not a coup because Trump did not order the military "to seize power on his behalf".[^cite_note-wRwC4-318][^cite_note-6OT6q-319] The Coup D'état Project of the Cline Center for Advanced Social Research at the [University of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Illinois), which tracks coups and coup attempts globally, classified the attack as an "attempted dissident coup", defined as an unsuccessful coup attempt "initiated by a small group of discontents" such as "ex-military leaders, religious leaders, former government leaders, members of a legislature/parliament, and civilians \[but not police or the military]". The Cline Center said the "organized, illegal attempt to intervene in the presidential transition" by displacing Congress met this definition.[^cite_note-ClintCenterCNN-320][^cite_note-ClintCenterStmt-321] Some political scientists identified the attack as an attempted [self-coup](1221872.md), in which the [head of government](37570.md) attempts to strong-arm the other branches of government to entrench power.[^cite_note-UiQHf-322] Academic [Fiona Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiona_Hill_\(presidential_advisor\)), a former member of Trump's [National Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._National_Security_Council), described the attack, and Trump's actions in the months leading up to it, as an attempted self-coup.[^cite_note-IDgK3-323] Experts have differed about whether the events on January 6 amounted to a coup or the like, some say yes,[^cite_note-ayeone-324][^cite_note-ayetwo-325][^cite_note-ayethree-326] some say no.[^cite_note-nayone-327][^cite_note-naytwo-328] News reporting about scholarly opinions reflects that division.[^cite_note-Zeitz-329]

The FBI classified the attack as domestic terrorism.[^cite_note-GHvJC-330][^cite_note-L4naB-331] At the Senate Judiciary Committee meeting on March 2, 2021, Wray testified:

> I was appalled, like you, at the violence and destruction that we saw that day. I was appalled that you, our country's elected leaders, were victimized right here in these very halls. That attack, that siege was criminal behavior, plain and simple, and it's behavior that we, the FBI, view as domestic terrorism. It's got no place in our democracy and tolerating it would make a mockery of our nation's rule of law.[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333]

The [Congressional Research Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Research_Service) also concluded that the attack met the federal definition of domestic terrorism.[^cite_note-RA0uX-334][^cite_note-uIhHG-335] Republican senator [Ted Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Cruz) characterized it as terrorism at least eighteen times over the ensuing year, though he was among the Senate Republicans who blocked a bipartisan [January 6 commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_6_commission) to investigate it.[^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337]

### Commemorations

Signs, flags, stickers, Pelosi's damaged nameplate, and other items left behind from the riot will be preserved as historical artifacts in the collections of the House and Senate, and the [National Museum of American History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_American_History), and "shared with national museums", including the [Smithsonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smithsonian).[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-Castronuovo-339] On the morning of January 7, military history curator Frank A. Blazich Jr. volunteered to go to the National Mall where he spent three hours collecting an array of objects discarded along the National Mall, including a large repurposed street sign which read "STOP THE STEAL – OFF WITH THEIR HEADS", along with discarded protest signs, American flags, and other pro-Trump paraphernalia.[^cite_note-340] [Anthea M. Hartig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthea_M._Hartig), director of the Smithsonian's National Museum of American History, said the Smithsonian would seek to collect and preserve "objects and stories that help future generations remember and contextualize January 6 and its aftermath", a statement echoed by Jane Campbell, president of the [Capitol Historical Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Capitol_Historical_Society).[^cite_note-iocue-341][^cite_note-BvBJF-342]

In March 2022, Congress mandated the display of a plaque honoring the police officers who were on duty at the Capitol during the attack.[^cite_note-plaque-343] After numerous unexplained delays, Congress missed its self-imposed deadline of March 2023 to install the plaque.[^cite_note-344] The plaque would have included the names of officers of the United States Capitol Police, the [Metropolitan Police Department of the District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Police_Department_of_the_District_of_Columbia), and other federal and state agencies.[^cite_note-345] It would have faced the west front of the complex.[^cite_note-plaque-343] On June 12, 2025, former U.S. Capitol Police Officer [Harry Dunn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Dunn_\(police_officer\)) and D.C. Metropolitan Police Officer [Daniel Hodges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Hodges_\(police_officer\)) sued over House Speaker [Mike Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Johnson)'s apparent abandonment of the project.[^cite_note-346] The Justice Department sought to have the lawsuit dismissed.[^cite_note-347] On the fifth anniversary of the riot, the Senate unanimously passed a resolution to display the plaque, which has a [QR code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QR_code) to view the names of the officers, at a temporary location in the Senate wing of the Capitol. It was hung in just inside an entrance on the West Front of the Capitol in March 2026. The area is not open to the public.[^cite_note-348] Later, it is to be moved to the west front of the Capitol, as specified by law.[^cite_note-349]

In December 2022, a Congressional Gold Medal ceremony was held in the [Capitol Rotunda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Capitol_rotunda), honoring the Capitol police and the Washington D.C. Metropolitan police; Democratic and Republican leaders from the Senate and House participated.[^cite_note-350] One of the medals is displayed in a glass frame in the Capitol Visitor Center, along with a list of 20 law enforcement agencies that were involved in the response to the attack.[^cite_note-351]

### Media documentation

An hour-and-a-half unedited documentary film titled *Full Video: The Siege On United States Capitol*[^cite_note-353] was produced at the scene, and released on YouTube, by [John Earle Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Earle_Sullivan), capturing many notable individuals and moments from the event, including the death of Ashli Babbitt. The author was deemed to be a participant to the civil disturbance and was charged with crimes.[^cite_note-354]

Some recordings from Capitol surveillance cameras and DC Metropolitan police body cameras were made publicly available in June 2021, following a CNN-led lawsuit by major media outlets. The Justice Department had argued that releasing recordings of current defendants could interfere with their right to a fair trial.[^cite_note-355]

A 2024 BBC documentary spliced together two moments in Trump's January 6 speech that in reality had been 54 minutes apart: his invitation "We're going to walk down to the Capitol" and his encouragement to "fight like hell".[^cite_note-356][^cite_note-357] An internal memo drew attention to the splice as improper. After the memo was leaked in 2025,[^cite_note-358] Trump's allies went on offense, and [BBC](19344654.md) director general [Tim Davie](1925670.md) and CEO of News [Deborah Turness](35601991.md) resigned on November 9. The next day, Trump accused the BBC of having tried to "interfere in the Presidential Election" and demanded that it give him \$1 billion within four days.[^cite_note-359]

#### Notable documentary films

- On June 30, 2022, *The New York Times* released *[Day of Rage: How Trump Supporters Took the U.S. Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Day_of_Rage:_How_Trump_Supporters_Took_the_U.S._Capitol)*, a 40-minute narrated documentary assembled from thousands of video and audio recordings from the event, much of the material recorded by rioters, with some being obtained through motions to unseal police body-camera footage.

  [^cite_note-360]

- In October 2022, [HBO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HBO) released *[Four Hours at the Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Hours_at_the_Capitol)*, a documentary by Jamie Roberts that details the events of the attack.

  [^cite_note-361]

  [^cite_note-362]

- In December 2022, HBO released *[This Place Rules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/This_Place_Rules)*, a documentary directed by the [Channel 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_5_\(web_channel\)) presenter [Andrew Callaghan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Callaghan).

  [^cite_note-363]

  [^cite_note-364]

- In January 2023, [Discovery+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discovery+) released *January 6*, a documentary by the French-American filmmakers [Jules and Gédéon Naudet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jules_and_G%C3%A9d%C3%A9on_Naudet).

  [^cite_note-365]

  [^cite_note-366]

  [^cite_note-367]

### First anniversary

Commemoration of the attack was seen leading up to and on the one-year anniversary in 2022 by right-wing groups, supporters of Trump, politicians, and the general public. President Biden gave a speech to commemorate the attack, in which he described the rioters as "holding a dagger to the throat of America" and claims that Trump held "singular responsibility" for the attack as "his bruised ego matters more to him than our democracy, our Constitution".[^cite_note-decries-368]

Many lawmakers observed a moment of silence at the House of Representatives chamber. A majority of Republican lawmakers were absent, excluding [Liz Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liz_Cheney) (R-Wyo) and her father, former Vice President [Dick Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Cheney), who told reporters that the current Republican leadership did not resemble anything that he had seen ten years prior.[^cite_note-369] Additional commemorative events were planned, including a moment of prayer, member testimonials, a prayer vigil, and a private event hosted by Pelosi, which thanked building staff for their protection during the attack.[^cite_note-decries-368]

There were protests planned to honor those who participated in the attack. A local chapter of the Proud Boys planned on holding a protest for all of those arrested afterwards, and an [anti-mask](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Face_masks_during_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_States#Attitudes) protest planned in [Beverly Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beverly_Hills,_California) was scheduled to be renamed after Ashli Babbitt.[^cite_note-370]

### Second anniversary

Biden marked the second anniversary of the attack in 2023 by holding an award ceremony, during which the [Presidential Citizens Medal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Citizens_Medal) was handed out to 14 people, some posthumously, who battled against the attackers.[^cite_note-371] The United States House of Representatives held a moment of silence to contemplate the attack.[^cite_note-372]

### Target letter to Trump and subsequent indictment

Main article: [Federal prosecution of Donald Trump (election obstruction case)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_prosecution_of_Donald_Trump_\(election_obstruction_case\))

On July 16, 2023, Trump received a target letter from the Department of Justice Special Counsel [Jack Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Smith_\(lawyer\)), who investigated the events of January 6.[^cite_note-trumpTargetLetter-373] Trump acknowledged that such a letter "almost always means an arrest and indictment" on his [Truth Social](69077351.md) account.[^cite_note-374][^cite_note-trumpTargetLetter-373] Following Smith's [special counsel investigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smith_special_counsel_investigation), an [indictment on four charges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_prosecution_of_Donald_Trump_\(election_obstruction_case\)) was unsealed on August 1, 2023.[^cite_note-375] These were [conspiracy to defraud the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_against_the_United_States) under [Title 18 of the United States Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Title_18_of_the_United_States_Code), [obstructing an official proceeding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obstructing_an_official_proceeding) and conspiracy to obstruct an official proceeding under the [Sarbanes–Oxley Act of 2002](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarbanes%E2%80%93Oxley_Act), and [conspiracy against rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_against_rights) under the [Enforcement Act of 1870](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enforcement_Act_of_1870).[^cite_note-376] Following Trump's reelection in November 2024, Smith filed a motion to dismiss the case without prejudice, citing the DOJ's policy of not prosecuting sitting presidents.[^cite_note-377] Smith reportedly plans to step down before Trump takes office.[^cite_note-378] On November 25, 2024, Judge [Tanya Chutkan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanya_Chutkan) approved the request and dismissed the charges.[^cite_note-379]

### 2024 election

In early October 2023, a poll by Survey USA found that 53 percent of Democratic voters, 45 percent of independent voters, and 42 percent of Republican voters expected serious violence after the [2024 presidential election](59568546.md).[^cite_note-380][^cite_note-381]

On December 6, 2023, at a [Republican presidential debate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Republican_Party_presidential_debates_and_forums), candidate [Vivek Ramaswamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vivek_Ramaswamy) asserted that "January 6 now does look like it was an inside job", by which he was falsely implying that it was orchestrated by federal agents.[^cite_note-382]

A Washington Post/University of Maryland poll conducted December 14–18, 2023 asked people whether they believed the protesters nearly three years earlier had been "mostly violent". Most answered yes: 77 percent of Democrats, 54 percent of independents, and 18 percent of Republicans. This response for Democrats and independents resembled previous polls, but for Republicans it showed a decrease (as 26 percent of Republicans had answered yes in 2021).[^cite_note-383] Polled on the same days, asked whether "FBI operatives organized and encouraged the attack", 34 percent of Republicans (and 44 percent of Trump voters) said yes, contrasted with 13 percent of Democrats (and 10 percent of Biden voters).[^cite_note-384]

At a press conference on February 8, 2024, Donald Trump used the word "insurrection" to refer to the attack, and subsequently alleged that "it was an insurrection caused by Nancy Pelosi".[^cite_note-385][^cite_note-386]

On June 13, Trump returned to Capitol Hill for the first time since the attack, meeting with Republican leaders at campaign headquarters. While Senate Minority Leader Mitch McConnell had ended in-person contact with Trump after the attack on the Capitol, he attended the meeting; it was their first in-person meeting together since the riot.[^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388]

Trump embraced and celebrated the January 6 Capitol attack and promoted a revisionist history of the event.[^cite_note-NYTJan6Embrace-15] He continually brought it up during rallies and speeches for his [2024 campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2024_presidential_campaign) and made it a political rallying cry.[^cite_note-AP_Jan_6\:1-16][^cite_note-AP_Jan_6\:4-389] He repeatedly called those charged for their actions on that day as "hostages" and "great, great patriots" and promised to [pardon them if reelected](78937582.md) (which he did immediately after his inauguration). He also called them "warriors" and "victims."[^cite_note-390] He downplayed the events of that day, calling it a "beautiful day" with "so much love," and played a video of the attack during a rally in [Waco, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waco,_Texas) with a choir of former January 6 rioters singing the song [Justice for All](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_for_All_\(song\)),[^cite_note-AP_Jan_6\:2-17][^cite_note-AP_Jan_6\:3-18] which also did at subsequent rallies and events.[^cite_note-391] He spread baseless conspiracy theories at his rallies that "there was Antifa and there was FBI" at the riot.[^cite_note-Politico_Jan_6\:1-392] *The New York Times* stated that his comments "risked radicalizing his most die-hard supporters even further, encouraging them to repeat events like those that unfolded on Jan. 6." University of Chicago professor [Robert Pape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Pape) stated that Trump's comments on the attack "normalizes violence as a legitimate solution to political grievances."[^cite_note-NYTJan6Embrace-15]

In September 2024, following a request from Washington, D.C., mayor [Muriel Bowser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muriel_Bowser), the [Department of Homeland Security](58236.md) designated the [January 6, 2025, electoral vote certification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_United_States_Electoral_College_vote_count) as a [National Special Security Event](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Special_Security_Event). This meant there would be extra planning and heightened security.[^cite_note-393]

On November 3, Trump told a crowd that "I shouldn't have left \[office]. I mean honestly, because we did so well."[^cite_note-394]

On January 9, 2025, at [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter)'s funeral, Trump and Pence briefly greeted each other, the first words they'd exchanged in years.[^cite_note-395]

Trump was [inaugurated on January 20, 2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_inauguration_of_Donald_Trump). Shortly thereafter, he began raising the idea of serving a third term, which is constitutionally forbidden.[^cite_note-396]

### Limit of Sarbanes-Oxley Act based prosecutions

On June 28, 2024, the U.S. Supreme Court issued a 6–3 ruling which ruled in favor of defendant Joseph Fischer and found that a section of the 2002 [Sarbanes-Oxley Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarbanes-Oxley_Act) could not be used to bring obstruction charges against the January 6 defendants.[^cite_note-397] Soon after the ruling, more January 6 prosecution cases would be reopened.[^cite_note-398]

### 2025 pardons and commutations

Main article: [Pardon of January 6 United States Capitol attack defendants](78937582.md)

On January 20, 2025, upon assuming the presidency again, Trump issued roughly [1,500 pardons and 14 commutations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_pardoned_or_granted_clemency_by_the_president_of_the_United_States) to people charged in connection to the [January 6 United States Capitol attack](66307490.md).[^cite_note-399]

### 2025 FBI leadership

Before Trump's inauguration, he announced he would nominate a new FBI director, and Christopher Wray resigned.[^cite_note-Maher_2025-400]

On February 21, 2025, [Kash Patel](62202727.md) became FBI director.[^cite_note-401]

On February 23, Trump announced that podcaster [Dan Bongino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Bongino) would be the next deputy director.[^cite_note-402] A month earlier, Bongino had said on his podcast that he believed the FBI knew who planted the pipe bombs outside DNC and RNC offices the day before the Capitol attack, and that it had been "an inside job" intended as a [false flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_flag) operation "to create a narrative that crazy MAGA people are trying to assassinate Kamala Harris".[^cite_note-403] Under Bongino's leadership, in December, the FBI made an arrest in the pipe bomb case that did not comport with the "inside job" theory.[^cite_note-404] Representative Barry Loudermilk, in his role as the chair of the House Select Subcommittee to Investigate the Remaining Questions Surrounding January 6, 2021, asked to interview the woman who had found the pipe bomb, reviving the "inside job" theory.[^cite_note-405]

On September 26, 2025, Trump accused Wray of "LYING". The next day, he said there had been FBI agents at the "January 6th Protest, probably acting as Agitators and Insurrectionists". He said he wanted the names of the "so-called 'Agents'," for whose alleged behavior "Many Great American Patriots were made to pay a very big price only for the love of their Country."[^cite_note-Maher_2025-400]

### 2025 consequences for federal prosecutors

By the end of February 2025, [Ed Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ed_Martin_\(Missouri_politician\)), the acting US attorney in Washington, DC, permanently demoted at least eight senior federal prosecutors to entry-level jobs. They had worked in the Justice Department's division of [Federal Major Crimes](https://www.justice.gov/usao-dc/federal-major-crimes) and in the section of [Fraud, Public Corruption and Civil Rights](https://www.justice.gov/usao-dc/fraud-public-corruption-and-civil-rights-section), and they had all worked on the Capitol riot cases.[^cite_note-406]

### Fifth anniversary



A commemorative plaque

[^cite_note-2026_PlaqueForJanuary6-407]

&#x20;honoring those who protected the US Capitol on January 6 sat for years out of sight in the Capitol basement, surrounded by maintenance equipment.

[^cite_note-WashPost_20260311-408]

&#x20;A 2022 law mandating the plaque stated it must be placed "at a permanent location on the western front".

[^cite_note-WashPost_20260311-408]

&#x20;In March 2026, more than five years after the attack, it was placed on a wall inside the Capitol, in an area closed to all tours and accessible by visitors only if they are escorted.

[^cite_note-WashPost_20260311-408]

On January 6, 2026, the members of the former [January 6 House select committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_6th_Committee) held a hearing to reexamine their findings. Simultaneously, the Proud Boys, including Enrique Tarrio, gathered to march to the Capitol.[^cite_note-409][^cite_note-410][^cite_note-411] The White House launched a webpage amplifying multiple false claims about the riot, praising Trump's pardons, claiming that Democrats "staged the real insurrection by certifying a fraud-ridden election", blaming Capitol Police for escalating the riot, and accusing Pence of "cowardice and sabotage".[^cite_note-412][^cite_note-413][^cite_note-414] Representative Jamie Raskin published an opinion piece in *The New York Times*, in which he said: "It's still very much Jan. 6 in America. ... Mr. Trump's incoherent revisionist mythology of Jan. 6 has become an organizing policy commitment of his administration."[^cite_note-415]

### Copycat crimes

On June 29, 2026, 29-year-old Shane Cautillo of [Boston](24437894.md), [Massachusetts](1645518.md), was charged with one count of possession of a hoax incendiary device, possession of a hoax device or substance, unlawful possession of fireworks, and disorderly conduct on a public conveyance after allegedly making bomb threats and bringing explosives onto an [MBTA Commuter Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MBTA_Commuter_Rail) train on June 27. A witness reported that Cautillo had made loud comments about making bombs and giving them to extremist groups, terrorist groups, and the homeless, in addition to stating that he was inspired by the January 6 attack.[^cite_note-416]

["Capitol assault a more sinister attack than first appeared"](https://apnews.com/article/us-capitol-attack-14c73ee280c256ab4ec193ac0f49ad54). Associated Press. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20210113193321/https://apnews.com/article/14c73ee280c256ab4ec193ac0f49ad54) from the original on January 13, 2021. Retrieved January 12, 2021. Under battle flags bearing Donald Trump's name, the Capitol's attackers pinned a bloodied police officer in a doorway, his twisted face and screams captured on video. They mortally wounded another officer with a blunt weapon and body-slammed a third over a railing into the crowd. 'Hang Mike Pence!' the insurrectionists chanted as they pressed inside, beating police with pipes. They demanded House Speaker Nancy Pelosi's whereabouts, too. They hunted any and all lawmakers: 'Where are they?' Outside, makeshift gallows stood, complete with sturdy wooden steps and the noose. Guns and pipe bombs had been stashed in the vicinity. ... The mob got stirring encouragement from Trump and more explicit marching orders from the president's men. 'Fight like hell,' Trump exhorted his partisans at the staging rally. 'Let's have trial by combat,' implored his lawyer, Rudy Giuliani, whose attempt to throw out election results in trial by courtroom failed. It's time to 'start taking down names and kicking ass', said Republican Representative Mo Brooks of Alabama. Criminals pardoned by Trump, among them Roger Stone and Michael Flynn, came forward at rallies on the eve of the attack to tell the crowds they were fighting a battle between good and evil

[^cite_note-ProvostHarvey-314]: &#x20;Harvey, Michael (2022). "Introduction: History's Rhymes". In Harvey, Michael (ed.). *Donald Trump in Historical Perspective*. New York: Routledge. p. 3. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.4324/9781003110361-1](https://doi.org/10.4324%2F9781003110361-1). [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-003-11036-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-003-11036-1). As with the Beer Hall Putsch, a would-be leader tried to take advantage of an already scheduled event (in Hitler's case, Kahr's speech; in Trump's, Congress's tallying of the electoral votes) to create a dramatic moment with himself at the center of attention, calling for bold action to upend the political order. Unlike Hitler's coup attempt, Trump already held top of office, so he was attempting to hold onto power, not seize it (the precise term for Trump's intended action is a 'self-coup' or 'autogolpe'). Thus, Trump was able to plan for the event well in advance, and with much greater control, including developing the legal arguments that could be used to justify rejecting the election's results.

Among the objects: a sign that read, 'Off with their heads: Stop the steal.' Other leavings, including pro-insurrection stickers and flags found inside the Capitol, will be preserved along with artifacts like the speaker's damaged name plate in the House and Senate collections and shared with national museums, including the Smithsonians, said the Committee on House Administration spokesperson.
